available to them eases. 
The risk is that what could be more of a rotation—and a flattening in the upward trend in 
asset markets—becomes a bigger aggregate shock. This could happen because the 
market begins to look further forward to the peak in the investment share itself (in 2028 
on our forecasts); because the passing of the baton to other sectors does not take place 
as quickly or as smoothly as needed; or because feedback loops from financial and 
credit conditions accelerate more quickly than in our baseline. We think the risk to 
markets is higher than the risk to the economic picture on each front, because the boom 
itself has had its largest impact there. For that reason, the most likely channel through 
which AI worries could lead to broader economic downside is through financial 
conditions themselves.

4. Energy—balancing risk and risk premium 
An escalation-driven oil spike remains one of the largest near-term risks to markets. n
Our baseline is for a gradual decline in energy prices, but the hedge value of energy n
is still high. 
Downside tail to energy prices with clearer/faster resolution. n
Risk premium has fluctuated, so hedge value also varies over time. n
A potential oil price spike driven by another escalation in the war with Iran remains one 
of the biggest near-term risks to the markets and growth picture. Particularly after the 
fall in oil prices so far in November, pricing is vulnerable to any broadening in the conflict 
and new supply disruptions. We would expect that to hurt equities, support the Dollar 
against European currencies and put upward pressure on rates, all else equal. Europe, 
too, faces a precarious winter, given low storage levels in natural gas which leave the 
region vulnerable to risks from both supply and weather. If those risks are avoided, our 
baseline is for a gradual decline in energy prices over the coming year even without full 
resolution of the conflict. Despite the ongoing war with Iran, oil market supply has 
largely been restored (even if refining capacity is limited), but risks of escalation and 
further disruptions have generally led the market to demand a fluctuating but 
meaningful risk premium above where we think the fundamental supply-demand picture 
would leave them.

### Page 7

Our baseline is a gradual recovery in supply and reduction in energy prices, leaving 
energy and core commodity returns more muted. But the risk of more serious 
disruptions is real, and energy prices could move significantly higher in those scenarios 
at a time when equities and, potentially, bonds could both be under pressure. The 
hedging value of the asset class remains high for that reason. The wrinkle is that the risk 
premium in oil has varied substantially and conditions the value of the hedge. When the 
risk premium falls, as it has done more recently, that hedging value is likely to be higher. 
When that risk premium is very high, as it was at the end of October, oil markets are 
more vulnerable to downside tails if news flow is better than expected, and outright 
longs (rather than options) have generally been more risky. The downside tail from faster 
progress towards resolution than we expect (not just in Iran, but in Ukraine too) is one of 
the clearest sources of upside risks for broader markets. In that scenario, energy prices 
could come down more quickly, supporting both bonds and equities.

5. Bonds reclaim macro and hedging value 
Rates moved up in 2026, on oil, hikes, AI spending and fiscal deficits. n
Central bank pricing now too hawkish relative to our baseline. n
Bonds should be a better hedge as supply shocks give way to demand stresses. n
Higher running yield, more paths to relief, and cheap valuations argue for higher n
bond allocations. 
Interest rates moved higher in 2026 after a low point on the eve of the Iran war. The 
resultant surge in energy prices and central bank pricing has been the primary catalyst 
across most DM and EM rates markets, but real rates, especially in the US, have 
continued to move higher as investment demand from the AI build-out interacts with 
sustained fiscal deficits. Despite that backdrop, we think that central bank pricing in 
many economies, including the US and the UK, is too hawkish relative to what is likely to 
be delivered. Even with the energy shock, the underlying inflation story is still fairly 
benign, which means that policy rates are likely to peak at lower levels relative to 
2023-2024 and reverse earlier. The risk to this view is that the energy shock extends 
further or inflation remains stickier and the labour market proves more resilient, as it did 
this year. 
Bonds have been a poor hedge in this environment of energy supply shocks, rate hikes

### Page 8

and real rate pressures from private and public sector spending. But we think the case 
for duration has shifted in a more constructive direction for several reasons. First, on a 
static basis, bond yields are now providing a good running yield and a decent spread to 
cash. Second, valuations are cheap (we see UST 10y yields at 4.6% by end-2027), 
positioning is underweight, and there are paths to some shallow rate relief from current 
levels under our benign baseline scenario, and scope for larger moves if AI spending 
decelerates more quickly. Third, the increase in yields has started to create stresses that 
indicate that a higher level of real rates may be difficult to sustain. Bonds are already 
starting to illustrate their value as a hedge against shocks of these types—with Bunds 
rallying as French sovereign spreads widened and USTs rallying as tech stocks came 
under pressure. Although energy spikes remain a near-term risk, we are more positive 
about bond allocations even after the recent rally, in part because of that hedging value.

6. A more cyclical Dollar, Asia’s revaluing currencies 
After tight ranges in 2026, lower inflation, energy prices make for a softer Dollar. n
But a bigger USD swing could come if US tech exceptionalism erodes. n
JPY is a more attractive hedge with ‘team Japan’ focused on limiting Yen weakness. n
CNY appreciation has further to run given undervaluation and a surging trade n
surplus.  
The broad Dollar is finishing 2026 on the strong side of tight ranges, buoyed by a sturdy 
US nominal growth backdrop, higher energy prices, and a shift to more hawkish Fed 
pricing. Under our baseline forecast, all three impulses should fade: alongside trend 
growth we expect inflation in the US to converge towards target, energy prices should 
decline as the Middle East disruptions gradually normalize and we expect the Fed to 
move from hikes to cuts as we head into late 2027 and early 2028. That should make for

### Page 9

a softer Dollar backdrop, and we have pencilled in a roughly 3% fall in the broad 
trade-weighted Dollar in 2027. 
But the real swing factor for FX is likely to be how the peak in AI-related tech spending 
affects US equity returns. Over the past several years, tech-related macro and market 
outperformance in the US has sucked in equity capital flows from the rest of the world 
and has been the primary factor supporting the Dollar’s premium valuation. Our 
baseline forecasts envisage a more even distribution of returns globally rather than US 
underperformance. But if US tech performance erodes meaningfully, that would likely 
come alongside some cyclical underperformance of the Dollar rather than a traditional 
‘safe haven’ bid, as we have already seen in short periods over the past couple of years. 
Instead, we would expect the undervalued Yen to prove a better haven in that scenario, 
given its typical sensitivity to equities and rates falling together. With Japanese policy 
authorities also now focused on limiting further Yen depreciation and encouraging 
inflows into Japanese assets, we think that the Yen should increasingly be a more 
attractive hedge to pro-cyclical portfolios. We also see scope for the Yuan to continue to 
appreciate gradually as China’s trade surplus keeps growing, and forward markets 
underestimate how far this process can run given the deep undervaluation starting 
point. While domestic demand is also very weak, we have argued that China needs both 
currency appreciation and expansionary policy, two instruments to hit two targets, to 
move towards better external and internal balance.

7. Europe & UK—global shocks and local shifts 
European markets have been under pressure from energy ToT, higher global yields n
and sovereign stresses. 
Even if global shocks abate, local concerns still persist until the French election, n
keeping EUR downside attractive.

### Page 10

UK equities are resilient and defensive, Sterling has continued to strengthen. n
UK Gilts may be clearer outperformers given their highest beta to oil prices. n
European macro markets have had a tough year, primarily reflecting a negative terms of
trade shock, rate tightening without an offsetting investment spending boom, and most
recently the reappearance of sovereign stresses, especially in France. Even with the
repricing, there are still challenges: the energy risks are likely to be most acute going

through the winter, and the underlying fiscal issues in France are unlikely to be
adequately addressed until the French election in the Spring of 2027. As a result, using
the Euro as a funder for other crosses has been a popular position, and the skew of risks
and low carry continue to make downside optionality attractive for the near term. While
Europe remains hostage to global shocks, the main paths to relief also run through those
global channels—energy relief would be most helpful, as would lower yields in the rest of

core markets. Together they would also lessen the immediate pressure on fiscal budgets,
even though the underlying primary deficit would still need to be addressed. Any
slowdown in AI-related spending may also show European assets in a better relative light
given a much smaller share of tech in the region’s equity indices.
In those scenarios, the UK may shine even brighter. Gilts have had the highest beta to oil
prices, so a reduction in those pressures may provide the most relief to UK rates and to

the fiscal picture there. UK equity indices also have less tech exposure than the US
(although they do embed significant energy exposure), and so may also prove more
defensive in an AI-related spending slowdown and any associated market pressures. And
while the currency may also benefit from a softer Dollar backdrop, it is already the most
overvalued currency within our G10 coverage. Much has gone right for the Pound in
recent months—a smooth transition to a new Labour government, a ‘do-no-harm’

budget delivery, the BoE shifting to hikes, and encouraging talk of a closer settlement
with the EU. But we think further gains are likely to be more challenging, with our
monetary policy forecasts most dovish to market pricing in the UK.
12 November 2026   10
Goldman Sachs Global Markets Outlook 2027

### Page 11

8. The stress of financing the boom 
AI investment financing has driven credit and equity issuance to cycle records. n
This has been digestible so far, but with discounted pricing needed in credit that has n
led spreads wider. 
A further widening in US spreads looks likely and a higher chance of meaningful n
widening than meaningful tightening. 
Europe offers more risk premium; mortgages and structured credit give some refuge n
from corporate supply. 
Credit and equity issuance has been hitting fresh cycle records, and the financing needs 
of the AI investment boom mean that those needs are likely to build further in 2027. So 
far, that issuance has—for the most part—been digestible, albeit with discounted 
pricing, and the credit underperformance that we expected in 2026 because of these 
dynamics has only really become clear in the last few months. Balance sheets of the 
major borrowers are strong and while free cash flow positions for the hyperscalers have 
deteriorated sharply, the overall corporate sector is much more balanced. We expect 
continued bifurcation between AI-related issuers and “everything else”, with episodic 
indigestion of jumbo AI debt financing leading to wider spreads for AI-related sectors.  
We expect further widening in index-level credit spreads in 2027, though in our base 
case the scope is fairly modest. The risks around that view are skewed though. It is hard 
to see scenarios where US credit spreads tighten sharply (there is more scope for 
tightening in Europe where the risk premium has increased more), and easier to see how 
spreads could move wider, particularly if economic conditions deteriorate more than we 
expect or if investors become more uncomfortable about the profitability of the AI 
investment boom. We expect modest US HY underperformance (decompression) in our

### Page 12

baseline, but the risk is that problems in the left tail of weaker borrowers create more 
turbulent conditions for broader financing. Mortgages and structured credit markets 
may offer some refuge from the heavy corporate supply. From 1998 to 2000, there was 
sustained widening in credit spreads and substantial decoupling between credit and 
equity markets. Without the large capital inflows and goods price deflation from the EM 
crises in 1997 and 1998, we think that kind of divergence will be harder now.

9. EM—resilience and diversification 
2026 is one more year when EM assets have shown resilience/outperformance in the n
face of external shocks. 
But the flip side is less compelling valuations and lower carry, despite friendly macro. n
So the case for EM investing is exposure to high, diversified and resilient yields in EM n
fixed income ... 
... and a regional and sectorally diversified earnings mix in EM equities.  n
EM assets have again demonstrated impressive resilience in 2026 in the face of shocks 
from high energy prices and the spikes in core rates. EM rates have outperformed core 
rates, EM FX total returns are outpacing G9 and EM equities are on track for a narrower 
but still double-digit year after 30% gains in 2025. The upshot of this resilience and 
outperformance is that in many places valuations are less compelling than before. EM 
local rate spreads are tight relative to core rates, EM credit spreads are the tightest in 
years despite attractive all-in yields, and EM FX carry and valuations are firmly 
middle-of-the-road. 
Even though the baseline macro backdrop looks friendly for EM investing and 
carry-harvesting in 2027, less attractive valuations and carry levels mean that it is about 
gaining exposure to high diversified yields in both hard and local currency fixed income. 
In many cases, such as South Africa, Hungary, Colombia and India, those higher yields 
come with improving fiscal and structural reform stories. Likewise, in the case of EM 
equities, while the major markets are not expensive, much of the value in the headline 
index is an artefact of the surge in AI earnings that will be hard to repeat. Rather, the 
case for EM equity length is also about gaining exposure to a diversified earnings mix.

The EM equity index, especially the less concentrated, equal-weighted flavours, provide 
exposure not just to the tech and AI complex in North Asia, but also to cyclical and 
commodity-related growth in South Africa and LatAm, and to larger domestic demand 
stories such as India. And if we see a larger move lower in core rates or the Dollar than 
our baseline expectations, EM local assets provide upside convexity that is harder to 
replicate elsewhere.

10. Growth risks and tail hedges 
A benign baseline, but growth risks now skew to the downside. n
Diversification benefits from bonds, commodities and equity selection. n
Equity index volatility has scope to rise further. n
Downside in rates, USD/JPY, upside in energy and protection in equity options. n
With a steady economic backdrop, our baseline forecasts for 2027 are for positive 
returns across equities and bonds. But with a maturing market cycle and significant 
uncertainty around core drivers of the outlook, we continue to emphasize the value of 
diversification and hedges to protect against key risks. Some of those risks—from higher 
oil prices or sticky core inflation—are likely to push rates higher. But a growing number—
a sharper reversal in the AI boom, cybersecurity fears, AI-related labour-shedding or 
financial stresses—would push both growth and rate expectations lower. The further we 
move into 2027, the more likely it is that the key risks are positive for bonds, as the 
threats from central bank hikes, energy price spikes and the AI investment tailwind 
recede. Diversification remains valuable in this environment. As we have said, the value 
of bonds as a diversifying asset is returning as the skew of risks shifts. And 
commodities—especially energy—continue to play a hedging role for portfolios, 
particularly when implied risk premia fall as they have recently.  
Alongside diversification, direct hedges are worth considering. Positioning for lower 
rates in the US and Japan, or for jointly lower rates and equities are potential ways to 
protect against deeper downside growth tails. In FX, USD/JPY downside may play a 
similar role, given that it generally performs well when rates and equities are both falling

lower volatility, akin to DM assets in recent years

Global Views: Downside Growth Risks
26 October 2026 | 6:45AM EDT
1. So far in 2026, global growth has been remarkably unremarkable.  Both the world 
as a whole and the G3 economies are growing in line with our estimates of potential 
GDP.  Our interpretation is that fiscal easing and the positive impulse from the AI 
boom have offset the negative impulse from the war in Iran and the lingering drag 
from higher US tariffs.

2. Our standing global GDP forecasts project more of the same in 2027, as lower 
energy prices offset fading tailwinds from fiscal policy and AI.  But the balance of 
risks is on the downside.  The most immediate concern is that, far from subsiding, 
energy prices have again climbed sharply on fears of renewed Middle East escalation.  
The prices of gasoline, diesel, jet fuel, and European/Asian natural gas are now near 
or even above their March/April highs, with likely negative effects on consumers and 
energy-importing economies more broadly.

### Page 2

3. Although we don’t expect significant fiscal policy changes in 2027, the consequences 
of large-scale deficits—coupled with AI-linked borrowing and central bank hikes—have 
started to show up more clearly in higher longer-term interest rates.  The moves have 
been mostly orderly, but the increase since early 2026 averages a sizable 100bp across 
DM yield curves.  If sustained, we estimate that this increase will subtract a cumulative 
0.6% from GDP via weakness in housing, consumer durables, and business investment.  
(Only part of the drag is incorporated in our standing forecasts because we expect bond 
yields to pull back as central banks deliver fewer hikes than priced in markets.)

Exhibit 2: Oil Price Risks Are Skewed to the Upside

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research
26 October 2026   2
Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 3

4. The growth impulse from the AI boom was set to peak in late 2026 or early 2027 even 
before concerns around the sustainability of the AI investment boom intensified in 
recent weeks.  Although AI investment is still rising rapidly, we expect the increase as a 
share of GDP to slow over the next year.  Alongside the fading boost from last year’s 
increase in US depreciation allowances, this will weigh on capital spending growth, 
especially in the US.  AI has also boosted US consumer spending growth via a positive 
wealth effect worth an estimated 0.4pp (or 0.25% of GDP) over the past year.  This 
impact is likely to decline over the next year, gradually if stocks perform in line with our 
strategists’ reasonably upbeat expectations but more sharply if AI-related stocks 
continue to decline.

5. Our own base case remains that AI investment will ultimately pay off in terms of higher 
productivity and economywide profits.  However, one key channel for this payoff is labor 
market disruption, which has negative near-term macroeconomic effects.  And while DM 
labor markets still look solid in aggregate, we are starting to see clearer signs that the AI 
impact is spreading beyond the technology sector.  Across nonmanufacturing industries, 
the share of US firms that have adopted AI in regular business functions now shows a 
statistically significant negative correlation with employment growth and a statistically 
significant positive correlation with unemployment.

6. The Fed’s recent signals—explicit from Williams/Jefferson and implicit from Warsh—
effectively rule out a hike on October 28.  But the FOMC still looks very likely to deliver 
another 25bp hike to 4-4.25% on December 9 because spot inflation remains too high.  
And while we continue to expect significant disinflation next year on the back of 
reduced impulses from tariffs, energy prices, and AI-related prices, the risks to our 
forecast are on the upside, most obviously in energy.  Higher inflation could result in

another hike in March, but either way we see renewed cuts in late 2027/early 2028.

7. Like the Fed, the ECB will likely hold rates steady next week and deliver a final hike (to 
2.75%) at the December meeting.  However, the turbulence in the French bond market 
poses somewhat greater downside risks to that call than in the US.  The turbulence has 
tightened financial conditions to a degree that is likely to shave ¼-½pp from annualized 
growth in coming quarters.  And if it intensifies into December, markets might perceive a 
hike as signaling insufficient focus by the ECB on the risk of Euro area fragmentation.  
While the ECB could address this issue by coupling a hike with renewed balance sheet 
expansion, the hurdle for such a move is high, in part because purchases of French 
bonds would inevitably raise concerns about moral hazard with respect to the needed 
fiscal adjustment.

### Page 6

November 5.  Consistent with our view that the UK economy is stronger than widely 
perceived, GDP growth has surprised on the upside this year and the unemployment rate 
declined to 4.7% in August on a 3-month average basis (with the volatile 1-month rate 
plunging to 4.1%).  Rising inflation and the looming household energy price reset in 
January imply a significant risk of further moves, although market pricing of 4+ hikes 
(including November) still looks excessive.  We also expect hikes in Canada and New 
Zealand this week and in Australia next week.

9. Slightly faster than expected growth in Q3 has pushed up our 2026 GDP forecast for 
China to 4.6%.  But most activity indicators are much weaker.  Both property activity and 
fixed asset investment are contracting year-on-year, and while nominal retail sales 
growth picked up to +1.1% in September, large retailers (whose sales are relatively 
better measured) are still posting substantial year-on-year declines.  With domestic 
demand rising slowly at best, sustained GDP growth in the official 4.5-5% target range 
will require ongoing steep increases in the trade surplus.  But the surplus is already at 1% 
of global GDP, and further increases will eat more noticeably into output and 
employment abroad.  Ultimately, China’s trading partners may respond by imposing 
trade barriers that weigh on Chinese exports and GDP, although the measures taken so 
far have been limited.

Exhibit 8: UK Growth Forecasts Have Drifted Higher

Source: Bloomberg, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research
26 October 2026   6
Goldman Sachs Global Views

### Page 7

10. Our strongest global view is that central banks will deliver fewer hikes than currently 
priced.  In addition, our rates strategists flag underweight positioning, diminished 
technical risks, and risk asset pressures as reasons why the recent rally might extend.  
The market’s narrow breadth makes our equity strategists wary of momentum factor 
exposure, but they still expect the S&P 500 to resume its climb after the midterm 
election, as earnings continue to grow solidly.  Our credit strategists remain cautious on 
IG technology bonds but see scope for some near-term reversal in HY given the scale of 
the recent selloff.  Our FX strategists expect the Japanese yen to act as a hedge against 
further AI jitters, in part because such jitters would shrink US-Japan interest rate 
differentials.  And our commodity strategists recommend targeted exposures to oil 
products and European natural gas to hedge near-term escalation risks alongside 
longer-term exposures to copper, gold, and select power markets to benefit from 
structural supply/demand imbalances. 
Jan Hatzius

EARNINGS AGGREGATES 
STOXX 600 Earnings Outlook provides analysis and commentary on aggregate earnings estimate revisions, growth rates and valuations.

Analyst: Tajinder Dhillon, CFA                                         November 5, 2026 
tajinder.dhillon@lseg.com

STOXX 600 EARNINGS OUTLOOK  
EUROPEAN AGGREGATE ESTIMATES AND REVISIONS

 Third quarter earnings are expected to increase 25.0% from Q3 2026. Excluding the Energy sector, earnings 
are expected to increase 9.5%.

 Third quarter revenue is expected to increase 11.9% from Q3 2026. Excluding the Energy sector, revenues are 
expected to increase 6.9%.

 212 companies in the STOXX 600 have reported earnings to date for Q3 2026. Of these, 58.0% reported results 
exceeding analyst estimates.

 257 companies in the STOXX 600 have reported revenue to date for Q3 2026. Of these, 64.2% reported revenue 
exceeding analyst estimates.

 During the week of November 9, 40 companies are expected to report quarterly earnings.

Through November 5, 212 companies in the STOXX 600 Index reported earnings for Q3 2026. Of these, 58% reported 
earnings above analyst estimates. In a typical quarter (sin ce 2012), 54% of companies beat estimates and 39% miss 
estimates.

In aggregate, companies are reporting earnings that are 4.2% above estimates, which is below the 5.9% long-term 
(since 2012) average surprise factor.

The estimated earnings growth rate for the STOXX 600 fo r Q3 2026 is 25%. The STOXX 600 expects to see share-
weighted earnings of €164.4B in Q3 2026, compared to shar e-weighted earnings of €131.6B (based on the year-ago 
earnings of the current constituents) in Q3 2025.

Eight of the ten sectors in the index expect to see an improv ement in earnings relative to Q3 2025. The Energy sector 
has the highest earnings growth rate for the quarter, wh ile the Real Estate has the weakest anticipated growth 
compared to Q3 2025.

The Energy sector has the highest earnings growth rate (153 .4%) of any sector. It is expected to earn €35.7B in Q3 
2026, compared to earnings of €14.1B in Q3 2025.

The Real Estate sector has the lowest earnings growth rate (-69.6%) of any sector. It is expected to earn €0.9B in Q3 
2026, compared to earnings of €3.1B in Q3 2025.

The estimated revenue growth rate for the STOXX 600 for Q3 2026 is 11.9%. The STOXX 600 is expected to earn 
revenues of €1581.6B in Q3 2026, compared to €1413.6B in Q3 2025.

Eight of the ten sectors in the index expect to see an improvement in revenues relative to Q3 2025. The Energy 
sector has the highest revenue growth rate for the quarter, while the Consumer Cyclicals has the weakest anticipated 
growth compared to Q3 2025.

The Energy sector has the highest revenue growth rate (39.4%) of any sector. It is expected to generate sales of 
€302.7B in Q3 2026, compared to sales of €217.2B in Q3 2025.

The Consumer Cyclicals sector has the lowest revenue growth rate (-1.2%) of any sector. It is expected to generate 
sales of €196.8B in Q3 2026, compared to sales of €199.1B in Q3 2025.

Document 392: New Zealand 2026 General Election_ Preliminary results and next steps (2026-11-19)

Source PDF: all report pdf/New Zealand 2026 General Election_ Preliminary results and next steps (2026-11-19).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-19
Pages: 9
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
Westpac New Zealand Economics
• As polls suggested, the election was a nailbiter
that has not yet been resolved. Election night
results show a lead to the centre-right on
paper in a significantly enlarged parliament.
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First
and ACT won 67 seats. The centre-left bloc
of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti Māori won 57
seats. There was a swing away from National
to its coalition partners while the centre left
grouping picked up a few seats.

• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling
and will enter parliament with at least 7 seats,
reducing what could have been a larger swing
to the centre-left grouping.
• The size of parliament will expand by around 9
seats as National held onto a large share of its
electorate votes, entitling it to more seats than
implied by its party vote share. As a result,
a bloc would require 66 seats to command a
majority in parliament.
• Several senior National ministers have not

been re-elected, including Finance Minister
Nicola Willis. She will continue in a caretaker
role but will need to be replaced in any new
centre-right government.
• Significant uncertainty about the result exists.
National might lose perhaps two marginal
electorates in the final analysis. There are
400,000 special votes to count which may
shift the list seat allocations as these tend
to reduce the share of the centre-right bloc
by 1.6-1.7ppts. Hence, when all is said and

done, the centre-right’s 10 seat advantage on
election night could reduce by 1-4 seats.
• Should this play out the centre-right would not
have sufficient seats to reach a majority in an
enlarged house and would require the support
of the Opportunity Party to govern.
• The final election outcome will not be
known until 27 November. Serious coalition
negotiations likely won’t begin until then
and delays could occur. Should recounts be
required, some further delay is possible.

• Based on these results, we see a higher
probability of a centre-right government
ultimately emerging that likely will require
the support of the Opportunity Party in some
form. The Opportunity Party likely won’t enter
Cabinet but may offer support on confidence
and supply in exchange for policy concessions.
• But a centre-left government can’t be ruled
out if special votes heavily favour the centre
left bloc, and especially if an agreement
between the Opportunity Party and the centre-

right parties can’t be reached.
• Regardless of the final count, the results
suggest a government with weak support and
ongoing vulnerabilities. Hence the prospect of
controversial large changes may be modest.
For a centre-right government that might
limit the extent of fiscal consolidation, while
the centre left’s plans for larger scale tax and
spending initiatives could be trimmed back.
• A centre-right government won’t change the
RBNZ mandate while a centre-left government

likely still will. A new finance minister will be
required with the possibility of ACT leader
David Seymour taking on that role.
ECONOMIC BULLETIN
New Zealand 2026 General Election:
Preliminary results and next steps.
9 Nov 2026 |  Westpac Economics Team | westpac.co.nz/economics | economics@westpac.co.nz
Kelly Eckhold, Chief Economist | +64 9 348 9382 | +64 21 786 758 | kelly.eckhold@westpac.co.nz

### Page 2

2
Westpac New Zealand Economics
The result.
The preliminary results of the 2026 General Election,
based on the election night count, are set out in the table
above. In summary:
• The centre-right bloc of National, NZ First and ACT
won 67 seats.
• The centre-left bloc of Labour, Greens and Te Pāti
Māori won 57 seats.
• The Opportunity Party delivered on its polling and will
enter parliament with 7 seats.
These results indicate a swing away from National to its

coalition partners while the centre-left grouping picked
up a few seats. The big gainer was the Opportunity Party
who perhaps took a big share of what otherwise could
have been a larger swing to the centre-left grouping.
The size of parliament will expand by around nine seats,
as National held onto a large share of its electorate
votes and hence has more seats than it would have been
entitled to based on its party vote share. As a result, a
governing bloc would require 66 seats to command a

majority in parliament.
Several of the electorate seats are very tight (see Table
2), so election night majorities could easily be overturned
once special votes are counted. Of particular relevance
is the seat of Wairarapa, where the National candidate is
leading the Labour candidate by just 139 votes. If Labour
were to win that seat (specials favoured Labour in 2020
but National in 2023), all else equal the centre-right seat
count would decline to 66 seats in what would become

a 130-seat Parliament – just enough to command a
majority. But if either NZ First or ACT were to lose a single
party vote seat to the left bloc once specials are counted
then that majority would disappear.
On that score, historically the election night results have
tended to overstate the support of the centre-right bloc
and understate the support for parties on the left of
the political spectrum. For example, over the previous
three elections (2017, 2020, 2023), the current centre-

right bloc’s vote share has declined 1.9%, 1.7% and 1.6%
respectively following the counting of special votes, with
the National Party losing 2 seats on each occasion once
the official results were declared (Labour, the Greens and
Te Pāti Māori have benefited). On this occasion, given its
overhang, a lower party vote share would not lower the
National Party’s final vote count, although it could alter
the size of its overhang and thus the number of seats

required to command a majority. In recent elections both
NZ First and ACT have seen their vote shares decline
following the counting of special votes.
The process from here.
Official results.
The official results will be declared on 27 November,
adding the results of an estimated 400,000 special votes
(with around a quarter of these cast overseas) to the
2.41m votes counted on election night.
We expect discussions between potential coalition
partners will begin immediately to start sketching out the

parameters of a coalition agreement. But given the tight
margins of the current vote count, no firm positions will
be agreed until the results are declared.
The writ and recounts.
After 27 November, the writ is scheduled to be returned
on 3 December – formally certifying who has been
elected. However, that date is subject to any recounts

### Page 3

3
Westpac New Zealand Economics
that might be requested. Parliament is required to meet
no later than 14 January (six weeks after the return of the
writ), providing an opportunity for a new government
to test that it has the confidence of the House. It is
worth noting that Parliament can be summoned and
begin functioning even if coalition negotiations are still
occurring. If no coalition or confidence-and-supply
arrangement has been reached by then, negotiations can

simply continue and the incumbent Government remains
in office under the caretaker convention (Ministers in
the outgoing government retain executive authority
temporarily, even if they are no longer MPs).
Given that the Pre-Election Economic and Fiscal
Update was released in late September there is no legal
requirement for the Treasury to publish a Half Year
Economic and Fiscal Update (HYEFU) this year. And given
that it will be some weeks before a government is formed,

it seems unlikely that there will be a government in place
that would request that a HYEFU be produced despite
there being no statutory requirement (certainly there
would be little time to incorporate policy changes that
fall out of new coalition agreements, thus diminishing
its value). Once a government is formed, it will have an
opportunity to lay out its immediate priorities in the
Budget Policy Statement (BPS), which by law needs to
be released before the end of March next year. The new

government could decide to release an interim fiscal
update with the BPS if that was thought necessary.
Potential coalition outcomes.
We see three main likely outcomes:
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First.
• A three-party majority coalition between National, ACT
and NZ First with the support of the Opportunity Party
on confidence and supply.
• Some coalition of Labour, the Greens, Opportunity and
Te Pāti Māori, potentially with Opportunity and Te Pāti

Māori sitting outside the cabinet.
Our preview of the election discussed the policies of
the main political parties, while the figure on page 5
highlights some points of commonality with the centre-
right and centre-left blocs.
National, ACT and NZ First share a broadly pro-
development and deregulatory agenda, including support
for greater resource development, fewer regulatory
barriers and compulsory or expanded KiwiSaver. Beyond
that common core, National and ACT align particularly

closely on lower taxes and deregulation, while National
and NZ First share an emphasis on infrastructure, regional
development and law and order. ACT and NZ First favour
tighter immigration settings and tighter welfare policies.
The differences are clearest at the edges: ACT advocates
deeper tax cuts, smaller government and more market-
led provision, while NZ First places greater emphasis
on economic sovereignty, regional development and
targeted support for working New Zealanders. National

occupies more of the middle ground, combining fiscal
discipline and a pro-business agenda with increased
infrastructure and housing supply.
The Opportunity Party presents itself as a middle
alternative that can work with the left or the right
groupings. They have five bottom line policies they say
they need to support any new government. These include
competition reform through enhanced Commerce
Commission powers, more restrictions on political
lobbyists and donors, a ten-year plan of investment in the

healthcare sector, an end to bottom trawling in inshore
areas and a phase out elsewhere and for the Government
to recommit to New Zealand’s Paris Agreement
obligations. Their success in meeting these bottom
lines with a new government and their flexibility around
these requirements will be key to their ability to form an
agreement with either side. There is better congruence
of the Opportunity Party’s policies with the left than the
right – so progress here will be critical to watch should

their support be required to form a new government. We
suspect they will be very motivated to avoid the need for
a new election via a hung parliament should push come
to shove.

Document 405: Productivity_ reasons for optimism (2026-11-19)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Productivity_ reasons for optimism (2026-11-19).pdf
Report source: Westpac IQ
Date: 2026-11-19
Pages: 15
Status: ok

### Page 1

#gdp #australia #employment #housing #rba #productivity
Productivity: reasons for optimism
By Pat Bustamante
Senior Economist, Westpac
08:37 November 17 2026 Share
The supply-side outlook is improving as capital deepening strengthens, reallocation drags
fade and AI diffusion gathers pace.
The supply-side outlook is looking brighter. A pickup in capital deepening and a fading ‘reallocation’
drag are already evident in the Annual National Accounts, with AI diffusion offering further upside.

This gives us greater conviction that productivity growth can return towards 1% or more over time,

### Page 2

Growth in 2024-25 was a little firmer than first thought
The 2025–26 figures show somewhat firmer economic activity and business investment than previously
estimated, particularly in 2024–25. The economy grew by 2.3% in year-average terms, broadly in line
with the initial quarterly National Accounts estimate of 2.4%. However, growth in 2024–25 was revised
up by 0.3ppts to 1.6%, largely reflecting stronger private business investment. Business investment is

now estimated to have increased by 1.1% in 2024–25, compared with the initial estimate of a 0.2%
decline. The revisions do not materially alter the broader productivity story, but they do suggest that the
economy was growing a little more strongly for the same rate of inflation in 2025.
above the RBA’s assumption and closer to Treasury’s. The recovery illustrates why it is important to
focus on longer-term trends in productivity growth rather than on the latest year or two, which can be

volatile.
Capital deepening is returning. Investment is picking up and, outside mining, market-sector capital
per hour worked is now at a record high, rising a strong 1.5% in 2025–26.
The reallocation headwind is easing. The shift of labour towards non-market industries, which
generally have lower measured productivity levels, has reduced the level of aggregate productivity by
around 1ppt over the past 15 years. With the expansion of the non-market sector plateauing, this

drag looks set to fade.
Productivity growth is about more than capital. Multifactor productivity, which captures how efficiently
businesses combine labour and capital, was the dominant driver of market-sector productivity growth
in 2025–26. Meanwhile, stronger investment in IT, utilities and professional services should provide a
further lift as new capital is put to work.

### Page 3

The investment upswing is broadening
The Annuals also provide a richer picture of the supply side, including investment by industry and the
evolution of the capital stock, details that are not available in the quarterly accounts. These data point
to a notable lift in investment in the market (ex-mining) part of the economy, where investment has
gone from around 10% of nominal GDP in to just over 11.5%. Outside of this, investment has been
softer, particularly in the mining sector.

### Page 4

Within the market sector, investment by the IT industry, increasingly focused on data centres,
has risen to its highest share of nominal GDP since the technology boom of the 1990s. Utilities
investment, including renewables and associated infrastructure, is stronger still, at its highest share
since 1983, while investment by the professional services industry has reached a record high.

### Page 5

Across the non-market sector (including health, education and public administration), investment has
also held at a higher share of the economy, driven by the health and public administration (including
defence) industries.

### Page 6

Investment is translating into capital deepening
The investment upswing is also beginning to translate into capital deepening as industries look to
rebuild capital stocks after a period of soft growth. Outside mining, market-sector capital per hour
worked is at a record high and well above its pre-pandemic level. That is an encouraging
development, particularly given that the sector accounts for 70% of total employment.
There are important divergences beneath the aggregate. Mining continues to experience capital

shallowing, as investment has been insufficient to cover wear and tear and capital requirements
associated with additional workers. By contrast, the capital stock in the non-market sector appears to
have caught up with the surge in employment we have seen since the pandemic, with capital per
worker increasing for the first time since the pandemic.

### Page 7

A major productivity headwind is starting to fade
Capital deepening will help support productivity growth across the economy. The Annuals show that
productivity in the market sector (excluding mining) continues to grow by around 1% in year-average
terms, while mining remains a drag on aggregate productivity (down 0.8% in 2025–26 and almost 30%
since 2017).

### Page 8

At the same time, a major recent headwind is easing as the rapid expansion of the non-market sector
approaches slows. This matters for aggregate productivity. Productivity growth reflects both gains
within industries, through capital deepening, technology and better processes, and the ‘reallocation’ of
resources between industries with different productivity levels.
Measured productivity in the non-market sector is considerably lower than across much of the market

economy, partly reflecting measurement issues (output in these sectors is generally not well measured,
particularly when there are quality changes). As employment shifted towards these industries, this
compositional effect weighed on aggregate productivity. Our estimates suggest it has reduced the
level of aggregate productivity by a significant 1ppt over the past 15 years and almost 0.3ppts
over the three years to 2025–26.

### Page 9

Productivity gains were about more than capital
It wasn’t only capital deepening that drove productivity growth. Multifactor productivity (MFP), which
captures how efficiently businesses combine labour and capital through changes in processes,
technology, know-how, was the dominant driver of market-sector productivity growth this year. This
gives us more conviction that the supply side is normalising, and productivity growth will improve from
its recent lows.

### Page 10

Where productivity is improving, and where it isn’t
At the industry level, some of those recording stronger investment are also beginning to deliver
stronger productivity outcomes, although the picture remains uneven.
IT stands out at the positive end of the spectrum, alongside improving outcomes in manufacturing,
administrative and support services, and transport.. These are also industries where the diffusion of
AI and other digital technologies could deliver further productivity gains. Utilities and

professional services should also benefit as stronger investment translates into greater capital intensity
and efficiency.
At the other end, mining remains a major drag, consistent with the capital shallowing evident in the
investment and capital-stock data. Construction is another notable laggard, with productivity continuing
to deteriorate despite strong demand from housing, population growth, renewable-energy infrastructure
and data-centre projects. Construction productivity fell around 2% in 2025–26 and almost 21% over the

13 years from 2012–13 to 2025–26.

### Page 11

A more constructive supply-side story
The Annuals provide a more constructive supply-side picture than the aggregate productivity figures
alone suggest. Market-sector productivity outside mining continues to advance, the reallocation drag
from the expanding non-market sector looks likely to fade, and capital deepening is strengthening
across important parts of the economy. The strong MFP outcome in the market sector gives us more
conviction that the supply side is normalising, and productivity growth will improve going ahead.

The critical question is whether the strong investment underway in technology, professional services,
electricity and related infrastructure translates into stronger productivity growth as that new capital is
put to work. If it does, the improvement in capital intensity evident in the Annuals could mark the
beginning of a more durable lift in Australia’s productivity performance.

### Page 12

Document 430: Three Questions About China’s Hidden Foreign Exchange Assets (2026-11-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Three Questions About China’s Hidden Foreign Exchange Assets (2026-11-12).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-11-12
Pages: 17
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
Three Questions About China’s Hidden Foreign
Exchange Assets
A trillion-dollar gap in foreign exchange purchases raises questions about where
China’s foreign currency assets are held.
Carlos Rawlins/Reuters
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
PUBLISHED
November 11, 2026 8:32 p.m.
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
The PBOC argues—unpersuasively, I think—that it
doesn’t have any hidden foreign exchange reserves. I
would reframe that as hidden foreign currency assets,
as foreign currency lent out (or swapped out) with
domestic financial institutions is a classic way of
masking reserves.
Many in Asia—and some in the IMF—are inclined
to believe the PBOC. Fair enough, it is hard to

challenge an institution as large and powerful (in
some ways) as the PBOC.

### Page 3

3
But to convince, the PBOC really needs to provide
answers to three questions.
1. What explains the current gap between foreign
exchange settlement and the PBOC’s reserves?
Settlement is the purchase of foreign exchange in the
market by the PBOC and the state banks. It
technically implies taking an open foreign currency
position, as the foreign exchange is being bought with
renminbi.
Historically, it correlated with changes in the PBOC’s
balance sheet.

And some gaps before 2012 can be explained. In late
2005 and 2006, the PBOC did over $100 billion in
swaps with the state banks, who used the FX to buy
foreign bonds (portfolio debt outflows surged). Those

### Page 4

4
swaps were unwound after the global financial crisis,
and actually provided some of the foreign currency
that “funded” the China Investment Corporation
when it initially scaled up.
And in 2007 and 2008, the PBOC required that the
state banks hold FX against their domestic RMB
deposits (to keep the FX out of reserves; it was held
on deposit at the PBOC and shows up as “other
foreign assets” on the PBOC’s balance sheet and as
“other, other, short-term” in the balance of payments.*

After 2018, though, there are unexplained gaps
between the FX bought in settlement and the
PBOC’s balance sheet (technically cumulative
monthly changes to capture the fact that FX is held as
its initial CNY purchase price).
There was a net accumulation of FX outside the
PBOC’s balance sheet in 2020 and 2021, when the
CNY was under pressure to appreciate. There are
modest sales in 2023 and 2024 when there is
depreciation pressure. And there are large purchases

in 2025 and the first 9 months of 2026—total
purchases in settlement in the last 12 months (spot)
are $500 to 600 billion, while the increase in the
PBOC’s balance sheet is only around $50 billion over
the same period.

### Page 5

5
The gap over time is now $1 trillion. The PBOC
needs to be able to answer a simple question: who
holds this FX, and who holds the associated FX
market risk (given the FX has been purchased with
RMB).
The monthly pattern of growth in settlement strongly
suggests it is backdoor intervention; there are sales
when spot is weak (economically) relative to the fix,
and purchases when spot is either at the fix or just
stronger than the fix (the strong edge of the band

hasn’t been used). The pattern of purchases and sales
thus looks exactly like that of a central bank
maintaining a tight band around a (crawling) peg.
If the PBOC cannot credibly explain this gap, there
isn’t much more to discuss. The PBOC argues that it
hasn’t swapped FX with the state banks or put foreign
currency on deposit at the state banks. Fair enough.

### Page 6

6
But someone is buying in settlement; the PBOC
ought to know who…
2. Missing interest income
There is an obvious tell here—Bloomberg reported
back in 2022 that the PBOC transferred $158 billion
in accumulated interest income to the Ministry of
Finance as part of a special dividend to hold the
budget deficit down. Again, fair enough. But
absolutely nothing changed in the FX reserves held
on the PBOC’s balance sheet or in the balance of
payments.
That highlights a broader issue. Since the end of

2016, the PBOC’s balance sheet FX hasn’t really
changed.

### Page 7

7
Peer institutions generally have an upward slope to
their reserves as a result of “gains from reserve
management” or “reinvestment of interest income.” **
The Central Bank of China (Taipei) and Japan’s
Ministry of Finance are good examples.
The interest on ~$3 trillion in reserves should be
around $100 billion a year. A bit less during the “low-
for-long” era before the pandemic, when SAFE wasn’t
getting much income out of its euro portfolio. A bit

more now, when U.S. short-term rates are well above 3
percent and SAFE’s legacy agencies carry yields of 5
or 6 percent. $100 billion over 10 years is real money
—roughly a trillion dollars.
If SAFE has steadily been selling its interest income,
it should say so (the fact that BOP reserves have often
diverged from the PBOC balance sheet suggests a
more complex story).

### Page 8

8
Moreover, if SAFE has been transferring the FX to
one of China’s many sovereign wealth funds
(including those controlled by SAFE) or the policy
banks, it should say so.
But SAFE cannot say what you see is what we really
have if it cannot explain how it handles the interest
on its now $3.5 trillion in reserves.
3. The accounting for the PBOC’s entrusted loans
and other foreign currency claims on domestic
financial institutions, notably the policy banks

FX handed over to a sovereign wealth fund to manage
doesn’t appear in reserves. It isn’t a liquid reserve asset.
But it should appear elsewhere in the balance of
payments as either portfolio debt or portfolio equity.
And FX handed over to the policy banks through
mechanisms such as entrusted loans (or a simple
deposit or swap) doesn’t count as a foreign reserve—
the foreign currency claim is on a domestic financial
institution, and that isn’t considered a “foreign” asset.

There is no doubt that the PBOC has domestic
claims on China’s financial institutions. The use of
“entrusted” loans to fund the policy banks in 2010
was reported by Caixin and the Financial Times.

### Page 9

9
$95 billion in entrusted loans were converted into
equity in the CDB and China Exim back in 2015
(giving SAFE majority equity stakes in both
institutions through its Buttonwood investment
vehicle).
And the SAFE’s 2020 annual report contained a ten-
year progress report on the “diversified use” of China’s
FX (basically, the non-reserve use) which indicated
that SAFE continued to provide entrusted loans to
small, medium, and large Chinese financial

institutions.
Now perhaps these instruments have been unwound
—the China Development Corporation (per Stephen
Green, a friend) has reduced its foreign currency
balance sheet over the last few years. But that should
lead the foreign currency to flow back to SAFE
absent another use—and that increase in FX isn’t
showing up in the PBOC’s reserves.
In other words, SAFE should provide a transparent
accounting of the FX that has been bought but
transferred to other financial institutions and any

ongoing foreign currency exposure to the domestic
banking system (including the policy banks).
These aren’t big asks. The settlement gap is big and
obvious, interest income is a known issue, and SAFE
itself has reported its use of entrusted loans in the
past. There is no issue around the past use of swaps

### Page 10

10
either. A substantial share of the banks’ foreign
currency assets are funded domestically. It should be
possible for SAFE to explain the role, if any, it has
played in this funding.
There is, of course, another issue around China’s
balance of payments: the growth in foreign currency
external assets funded out of domestic foreign
currency deposits.
China argues that the growing foreign assets of its
state banks are simply a natural part of a growing

domestic financial system. But there is more to the
story.
Onshore FX deposits have tended to rise when
economics say they should not, and fall when the
economics say they should (offshore FX deposits
behave as expected). In 2020 and 2021, FX deposits

### Page 11

11
rose even when there was more yield on RMB
deposits.
And RMB deposits have continued to rise in 2025
and 2026 even with the RMB’s appreciation
offsetting the higher dollar deposit rate. This isn’t an
accident: banks just raised the interest rate that they
pay on dollar deposits to encourage exporters NOT to
convert their export proceeds into RMB and thus
take pressure off the PBOC (per Reuters).
In other words, the pattern of the banks’ foreign

currency asset growth suggests that they are offsetting
appreciation pressure by bidding for FX (or receiving
it from other state actors), not that FX flows into the
banks during periods when onshore residents worry
about depreciation.

### Page 12

12
Right now, the “settlement gap” is the most pressing
issue. FX deposit growth in excess of FX loan growth
can explain FX outflows of around $150 billion in the
four quarters to Q3. The FX outflows implied by
settlement reached $600 billion over the last four
quarters. Together they account for the bulk of
China’s reported $825 billion plus (I estimate a Q4
2025 to Q3 2026 surplus of around $900 billion) in
outflows implied by the reported current account.***

### Page 13

13
It goes without saying that this matters because the
weak RMB is the central reason for China’s massive
export outperformance since 2023—and that export
outperformance has continued in 2026.****
* I have been doing this a long time; I actually do
know most of the historical quirks in China’s balance

### Page 14

14
of payments data.
** The MoF can count a portion of its unrepatriated
gains from accumulated interest on its large reserves
against the budget deficit, but since the actual
interest is held abroad, it still needs to borrow
domestically against those reserves (domestic JGB
issuance and foreign reserves both go up).
*** My estimate of a larger “true” current account
comes from moving gold imports over to the financial
account, and from an assumption that undercounting

of the reinvested earnings of funds held offshore
explains the obvious problem in the investment
income account.
**** The spike in imports in Q1 wasn’t entirely real.
China was slow to adjust its import price index for
the rise in semiconductor/chip prices. This inflated
both import and export volumes in Q1. The rise in
gold imports this year also registers in the import
volume series—though the rise is best understood as
“importing” a financial asset.

### Page 15

15

利差優勢消長，新興與商品
貨幣走勢各異
熱門議題 / 投資關鍵
高油價、美元偏強及全球景氣分化持續牽動新興與
商品貨幣。澳洲就業轉弱、通膨改善，澳洲央行升
息週期料暫告一段落，澳幣短線料區間盤整。紐西
蘭央行意外按兵不動削弱紐幣短期動能，但升息預
期仍提供支撐。加拿大央行政策轉鷹及高油價利好
加幣，惟經濟與貿易不確定性限制升值空間。中國
內需偏弱，但出口暢旺、企業結匯需求強，加上政
策對升值容忍度提高，人民幣料溫和升值。南非幣
受美元、高油價及利差收窄壓抑，但財政紀律改善、
信用前景轉佳及實質利率優勢有助緩和貶值壓力，
料維持區間震盪。
市場投資展望
資產 4Q26看法 投資展望

《投資關鍵 – 澳洲景氣放緩、澳央轉向觀望，預期
澳幣盤整》
隨著國際油價維持高位，澳洲央行（RBA）於9月再
度升息將現金利率提高至4.6%，官員雖表示若通膨
壓力持續，不排除進一步緊縮。
但隨著高利率與高通膨壓力持續拖累經濟動能，近期
公布的9月就業意外減少3.93萬人、失業率更升至
4.7%，已連續兩個月落在澳央主席認為可有效壓抑
通膨的失業率4.5~5.0%區間；通膨方面，9月份澳洲
CPI雖由4.0%升至4.3%，但核心CPI僅有2.2%，加以
住房通膨明顯回落，因此本行預期澳央轉向觀望、本
輪升息暫時告終，2027年政策轉向觀望，如國際油
價回落至90美元以下，澳央有降息空間。
外需方面，中國經濟基本面持續低迷，消費與投資不

振亦難以挹注澳洲出口動能，外需對澳幣支撐力量有
限，加以預期聯準會在12月再度升息，將呈現「利
差有撐但優勢遞減」格局，因此預估澳幣短線維持在
0.7~0.71區間盤整，要進一步突破或要待2Q27美國
通膨回落、降息預期再度升溫，方有走揚力量。
重點摘要
• 經濟基本面降溫，核心CPI穩
定，澳洲央行暫停升息轉向
觀望，澳幣維持在0.7~0.71
中性盤整。

紐西蘭通膨升溫訊號浮現，月度物價年增 5.2%
《投資關鍵 - 央行意外按兵不動拖累紐元，後續升息
預期提供下檔支撐》
過去一個月，受美元強勢、美債殖利率及油價走高拖
累，紐幣兌美元一度下探0.5565，其後隨升息預期升
溫而回穩。惟紐西蘭央行10月28日意外維持OCR於
2.75%不變，促使市場下修短期升息預期，紐幣再度
承壓。
紐西蘭經濟延續溫和復甦，企業信心及農產品價格改
善，惟勞動市場仍有閒置產能。雖然整體通膨低於預
期，但核心通膨及價格廣度上升，加上油價高企與紐

幣偏弱，我們預期4Q26通膨仍具黏性，價格壓力擴散
風險猶存。
我們仍維持偏鷹派的政策基準情境。10月以4比3票按
兵不動，較反映升息時點後移，而非整體政策路徑轉
向，緊縮週期尚未結束。我們仍預期12月及明年3月具
升息空間，尤其若通膨、油價及匯率壓力持續，政策
立場料維持偏緊。
展望4Q26，10月意外按兵不動削弱紐幣利差支撐，我
們仍看好紐元中期修復空間，惟短期上行動能有所減
弱。升息預期降溫，加上大選、美元及全球風險情緒
等不確定性，料使匯價維持較高波動。進入1Q27後，
隨政策不確定性逐步下降，經濟溫和復甦有望成為紐
幣主要支撐。考量匯價已反映相當程度的成長疑慮及
外部利空，若基本面持續改善，紐幣中期仍具修復空
間。

通膨數據回落，6月降息的可能性加大重點摘要 《投資關鍵 - 央行轉鷹支撐加幣，惟經濟及貿易風險
限制升值空間》
能源價格高企令加拿大通膨面臨上行風險，加拿大央
行政策預期轉鷹亦推動加美利差劣勢收窄，支持加幣
自10月中旬低點反彈。不過，隨本次25個基點升息
已大致反映於市場定價，加幣能否延續升勢將更取決
於央行對後續緊縮路徑的指引，以及市場對終端利率
的重新定價，而非本次升息本身。
加拿大央行最新升息25個基點至2.5%，為逾三年來
首次升息，並表示若能源價格進一步推升廣泛通膨壓
力，不排除繼續收緊政策。央行亦將2027年通膨預
測由2.0%上調至3.1%，並將今年經濟成長預測由
0.7%上調至1.0%，短期政策訊號偏鷹。惟核心通膨
仍相對溫和，勞動市場、製造業需求及招聘意願亦呈
轉弱跡象，意味若經濟活動進一步降溫，持續升息空
間或有限。高油價及偏高的投機性加幣空頭部位仍構
成短期利好，但美加貿易不確定性可能繼續拖累企業
投資及成長前景。因此，我們維持4Q26至1Q27加幣
中性看法。短期加美利差收窄及油價高企有望限制加

幣下行風險，但在增長前景改善或央行釋出更明確的
持續緊縮訊號前，加幣進一步升值空間有限。

### Page 5

，6月降息的可能性加大重點摘要
• 從景氣趨勢、政策取向與國
際經貿局勢變化三大視角切
入，綜合判斷人民幣溫和升
值的看法不變。
《投資關鍵 – 政經拉鋸與景氣K型下，人民幣溫和升值》
中國公布最新3Q26 GDP成長4.5%，出口年增率升至
28.4%，貿易順差由前季的3148.6億美元擴大至3651.2
億美元。市場預估中國2026 年全年貿易順差將超越
2025年1.2兆美元紀錄，再創新高。惟中國外需繁榮卻
無法掩蓋內需疲軟困境，中國3Q26零售銷售年增率僅
0.7%，固定資產投資衰退7%。儘管中國陸續推出穩增
長政策，大多以財政政策為主軸，貨幣政策偏向定向式
而非全面降準降息。因此儘管美啟動升息，中美利差尚
保持穩定，因此內需疲軟與有限度的寬鬆貨幣政策對人
民幣走勢並未形成顯著壓力。
中國的出口暢旺除受惠科技週期貢獻外，強勁汽車出口
至歐洲也是主因，9月中國汽車出口維持62%的高成長。
惟加劇保護主義傾向，中歐達成初步協議，未來四年內
將對歐洲的混合動力車出口量減少一半以上，具體配額、

最低售價及豁免條件仍待協商。政策角度觀察，人民幣
升值本身可能成為中國應對外部壓力的重要工具，減緩
低估匯率促進出口的國際批評。
考量關稅可能成為攻防戰手段之一，研判中國廠商或搶
出口支持外貿表現高檔，同時官方可能對人民幣升值空
間容忍態度提高，疊加前三季結售匯順差高達3,941億
美元，創2010年以來新高，顯示企業結匯意願強勁，持
續為人民幣提供支撐。從景氣趨勢、政策取向與國際經
貿局勢變化的三大視角切入，綜合判斷人民幣溫和升值
的看法不變。
【注意事項】本行所提供之資訊僅供參考用途。本行當盡力提供正確之資訊，所載資料均來自或本諸我們相信可靠之來源，但對其完整性、即時性和正確性

### Page 6

《投資關鍵 - 基本面改善但部位趨擁擠，南非幣料維
持震盪》
南非幣第四季以來震盪偏弱，USD/ZAR近期回升至約
16.60。外部環境仍偏不利，高油價推升進口成本與
通膨壓力，加上美元偏強及美國南非利差收窄，削弱
南非幣套息吸引力。與此同時，巴西總統大選結果引
發市場波動，增添新興市場不確定性，短期投資人風
險偏好轉趨審慎，亦可能加重新興市場貨幣的資金流
出壓力。
基本面方面，南非財政紀律改善、主權信用前景轉佳，
加上較高實質利率及具吸引力的利差，仍為南非幣提
供支撐。惟高油價及聖嬰現象相關極端天氣風險令通
膨壓力偏高，限制政策寬鬆空間。另一方面，近期投
機性及資產管理機構多頭部位明顯增加，顯示市場部
位漸趨擁擠。在外部風險升溫及風險偏好轉弱下，獲
利了結與部位調整可能放大短期波動，即使匯價回檔，
逢低布局的風險報酬吸引力仍相對有限。
整體而言，南非幣基本面有所改善，但短期外部環境
與部位面均偏不利。財政紀律改善、主權信用前景轉
佳及實質利率優勢有助建立匯率下檔支撐，惟強勢美
元、高油價、利差收窄及偏擁擠的多頭部位限制升值

空間。預期4Q26美元兌南非幣仍以區間震盪為主，維
持南非幣中性看法。
重點摘要
資料來源：CFTC，Bloomberg ，2026/9，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10

（注）  作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所経済調査部が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対す
る責任は負いません。
日本銀行の金融政策決定会合とは、総裁を筆頭とした９名で構成され
る政策委員会による日本の金融政策運営を検討・決定する会合です。年
に８回、各会合ともに２日間にわたって開催され、終了後に公表される決
定内容や金融情勢に関する基本的見解のほか、日銀総裁の会見にも市
場の関心が集まります。
直前の10月会合では政策金利が据え置かれたものの、日銀は消費者
物価の基調的な上昇率が目標とする「２％」の水準と概ね整合的になった
との見解を示しました。2013年の目標導入から長年続いた金融緩和は
「２％に近づける」段階を終え、金融政策は、経済情勢に合わせて緩和度
合いを調整しながら「２％程度で安定させる」新たな局面に入ったと言え
ます。
こうした流れを受け、12月会合では政策金利を1.50％へ引き上げる
追加利上げに踏み切るかが最大の焦点となっています。判断の材料としては、会合直前に公表される「日銀短観」におけ

る企業の設備投資計画や販売価格への転嫁動向、さらに翌年の春闘に向けた賃上げモメンタムの強さなどが総合的に
見極められる見通しです。２％程度の物価上昇を定着させ、物価目標を達成できるのか、日銀の今後のかじ取りに注目が
集まります。 （主席エコノミスト ： 阿原健一郎）

Document 585: 内外経済ウォッチ『欧州～フランスで広がる怒りの正体～』(2026年12月号)

Report source: DLRI

### Page 1

欧州
～フランスで広がる怒りの正体～
フランス各地で大規模なデモが続いている。10月には
一部が暴徒化し、商店の略奪や建物への放火など、衝撃
的な映像が世界に伝えられた。しかし、暴力行為の激しさ
と、フランス国民の怒りの大きさは必ずしも一致しない。
フランスでは、国民が政治に意思を伝える手段として、デ
モやストライキが定着している。混乱に乗じて破壊や略
奪行為を行う黒ずくめの集団は、平和的に抗議活動をし
ている多くの市民と目的を共有しない。
今回の抗議活動は、教育環境の改善を求める高校生た
ちの訴えから始まった。フランスの教育予算はドイツや日
本を上回り、教育環境が先進国の中で際立って劣悪なわ
けではない。だが、一部の学校では深刻な教員不足や校
舎の老朽化といった問題を抱えている。2027年予算案
では、少子化に合わせて正規教員のポストを減らした一
方で、教員不足への対応策として、教員を目指す研修生
や代替教員のポストを大幅に増やし、全体としては教育予
算を拡充する計画だった。
問題はそうした政府の政策が国民の間で十分に理解さ

れなかったことだ。抗議の声を受け、政府は正規教員の削
減方針を撤回し、学級規模の縮小や大学入試制度の見直
しなどを発表したが、国民の怒りは収まっていない。背景
にあるのは、政府に対する不信感の高まりだ。
教育問題を機に噴き出す国民の不満
フランスは、国民の税や社会保険料の負担が先進国の
中でも重い国の一つだ。それにもかかわらず、教育や医療
など身近な公共サービスが十分に行き届いていないとの
不満が根強い。物価高によって暮らし向きが厳しくなる一
方で、政府は財政再建を理由に歳出の抑制を進めようとし
ている。これだけ税金を払っているのに、なぜ必要なサー
ビスが受けられないのか。国民の怒りの根底には、そうし
た不公平感がある。
マクロン大統領はフランスの競争力回復を目指して改
革を進める一方で、国民の声に耳を傾けないエリート政治
家との批判を受けてきた。とりわけ年金の受給開始年齢を
引き上げる改革は、国民の強い反発を招いた。今回の抗議
活動は、教育問題をきっかけに、長年積み重なってきた政
治や社会への不満が一気に噴き出したものといえる。

こうした国民の怒りは、来年春に予定される大統領選挙
にも影を落としている。世論調査では、移民規制の強化や
自国民の利益を優先する政策を訴える右派政党「国民連
合」のルペン氏が高い支持を集めている。一方、今回の抗
議活動に積極的に関わってきた急進左派にも支持が集ま
る。右派と左派で主張は大きく異なるが、既存の政治に対
する国民の不満が支持の背景にあるという点では共通し
ている。
大統領選挙に影を落とす政治不信
経済調査部　首席エコノミスト　田中 理（たなか おさむ）
資料1 公的教育支出の対GDP比率（2020年、％）

動態更新 面板 ‧ 台灣

元太 
(8069.TW/8069 TT)

跨足 AI 伺服器 PTFE 應用，打造營運新引擎 
重要訊息 
元太舉辦線上法人說明會更新營運近況。 
評論及分析 
3Q26 毛利率低預期，2026 營收成長由 10-15%下修至 9%；2027 
Signage 與 ESL 持續向上，消費性電子視新品上市狀況。(1)毛利率 52.2%
低共識 58.2%，主要 3Q26 為模組出貨旺季（模組毛利率相對低於材料）所
致，消費型產品 CE (E-note & reader) 季增 7 個百分點佔比達 45%，IoT(ESL)
比重季增至 55%。(2)2026 消費性電子年減約 15%， IoT（含 Signage）呈
現 35% 以上年成長。(3) Signage 目前佔總營收高個位數，隨價格下探未來
出貨量將持續放大。(4)2026 營收與淨利皆將創新歷史新高。 
跨足 AI 伺服器新材料 PTFE 將帶來營運商機。由於軟板與電子紙薄膜均走 
捲對捲製程，元太在觀音除新建新廠外，另向華紙(1905 TT, NT$ 11.45, 未
評等)購買既有廠房與土地，建立完整觀音製造基地，將與全球 FCCL 第一大
廠台虹(8039 TT, NT$217, 未評等)合作(元太對台虹持股比例約 15.7%)，由
台虹提供技術與材料，元太在觀音廠提供空間、設施並進行幫代工與材料改
質合作，將核心塗佈與材料技術延伸至 AI 高速傳輸 PTFE 材料 開發，預計 
2027 年開始有少許營收貢獻，2028 年有機會正式放量。 
投資建議 
儘管 3Q26 季報低預期，我們持續看好中長期 ESL 與 signage 穩定成長趨勢
不變，而跨足 AI 伺服器新材料 PTFE 將為 2027-28 年帶來極大營運商機(尚
未計入本次財務預估)，維持「增加持股」的評等，調降目標價至 210 元，
相當於 2027 年每股盈餘 18 倍本益比。 
投資風險 
終端需求不如預期，政策或標案性等波動過大的影響。

圖 4: 公司概況  圖 5: 季產品組合 
元太科技成立於 1992 年，為全球第一大電子紙製造廠，以電泳
式電子墨水技術生產電子紙顯示器，為永豐餘集團子公司。公司
於 2009 年併購具電子墨水技術和電子紙量產的 E Ink( 美)，於
2016 年淡出 LCD 業務。2025年產品營收比重為消費性電子應用
56%和物聯網應用 44%。

核心观点：
从全国规模以上工业增加值看，10 月同比实际增速为 4.8%，低于 Wind预期 5.3%，
较 9 月回落 0.7个百分点；10 月季调环比为 0.21%，较 9 月 0.61%也有所回落；从
累计增速看，1-10月工业增加值同比实际增速为 5.3%，与前三季度持平。
从工业企业产销率看，10 月为 96.5%，较 9 月回落 0.5个百分点；而从累计值看，
1-10月为 96.4%，与前三季度季度持平。从出口交货值同比看，10 月为 18.6%，较
9 月回升 1.6 个百分点；而从累计值看，1-10月为 11.6%，较前三季度回升 0.9个
百分点。可见，1-10月中国经济的平稳运行，主要还是得益于出口的强力拉动，而
从内需看相对较弱。

从社会消费品零售总额的名义增速看，10月同比为 0.4%，低于 Wind预期 1.1%，较
9 月回落 0.7个百分比；而从累计值看，1-10月为 1.0%，较前三季度回落 0.1个百
分点；从季调环比看，10 月为 0.02%，较 9 月 0.34%也有所回落。
从固定资产投资的累计增速看，10 月同比为-8.1%，低于 Wind预期-6.9%，较前三
季度季度回落 0.9 个百分点。其中，制造业投资为-2.9%，较前三季度回落 0.8 个
百分点；基建投资为-7.3%，较前三季度回落 1.2个百分点；而房地产投资为-21.6%，
较前三季度回落 1个百分点。
从总体上看，2026 年 10 月中国经济数据低于预期，但结构分化明显，高科技产业
与传统产业间的差距越来越大。首先，从工业增加值上看，1-10月制造业的实际同
比增速为 5.7%，与前三季度持平；而高科技产业为 14.6%，较前三季度回升 0.3 个
百分点。其次，从零售名义增速看，以通讯器材为代表的必需消费品要高于以汽车
为代表的耐用消费品。最后，从房地产数据看，房地产价格虽有所企稳，但 1-10

月除房屋开工外的其它数据均低于前三季度。
风险提示：
对上述事件发展趋势的点评，存在由于经济增长、行业竞争、外部经济环境和政策
等变化，而不如预期的可能。

一、10 月整体经济仍呈现外需强而内需弱的格局
从 10 月全国规模以上工业增加值看（表 1 所示），10 月同比增速为 4.8%，低于市
场预期（Wind）5.3%，较 9 月回落 0.7 个百分点；10 月季调环比为 0.21%，较 9 月 0.61%
也有所回落；从累计增速看，1-10 月工业增加值同比增速为 5.3%，与前三季度持平。
从工业企业产销率看，10 月为 96.5%，较 9 月回落 0.5 个百分点；而从累计值看，
1-10 月为 96.4%，与前三季度季度持平。
从出口交货值同比看，10 月为 18.6%，较 9 月回升 1.6个百分点；而从累计值看，1-10月
为 11.6%，较前三季度回升 0.9个百分点。因此，1-10 月中国经济运行平稳，主要还是得益
于出口的强力拉动，而从内需看相对较弱，且由于假期因素 10 月较 9 月有所回落。

二、10 月信息等高科技产业与房地产等传统产业两极分化明显
从总体上看，2026 年 10 月中国经济数据低于预期，但结构分化明显，高科技产业与传统
产业间的差距越来越大。首先，从工业增加值上看，1-10 月制造业的实际同比增速为 5.7%，与
前三季度持平；而高科技产业为 14.6%，较前三季度回升 0.3个百分点。
图 1 2026年 10月制造业各行业实际同比增速与前三季度的对比
资料来源：国家统计局，第一创业证券整理

算机、通信和其它电子设备（19.4%），专用设备（11.5%），铁路、船舶、航空航天和其他运
输设备（9.2%），食品（8.6%）和通用设备（8.5%）。排名后五的行业包括：橡胶和塑料制品
（1.2%）、汽车（0.9%）、黑色金属冶炼及压延加工业（-1.7%）、非金属矿物制品业（-2.3%）

其次，从下游消费结构看，10 月服务业消费要远强于零售（表 1 所示），零售中非耐用品

关的大额耐用消费、以及可选消费的谨慎态度。10月与前三季度相比，名义同比增速大都低前
前三季度，显然是受到国庆假期的影响，只有体育娱乐用品类、家具类、家用电器和音像器材
类、石油及制品类，受益于黄金周名义同比增速较前三季度有所上升。

工面积同比增速为-26.6%，较前三季度回落 1.5 个百分点；房屋销售面积同比增速为-13.7%，
较前三季度回落 0.5 个百分点。

Document 738: 米中間選挙で民主党が大勝 ～出口調査からみる共和党の敗因～

Source PDF: all report pdf/米中間選挙で民主党が大勝 ～出口調査からみる共和党の敗因～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-04
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

US Trends

2026年 11月 4日

1/3 
米中間選挙で民主党が大勝 
～出口調査からみる共和党の敗因～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  前田 和馬 
（℡：050-5473-3888）

11 月3 日に投開票を迎えた米国中間選挙において、民主党が上下院の過半数獲得を確実とし
た。New York Timesの4 日午前 3 時（米東部時間）時点の予測では、民主党の議席数は上院で 55
（選挙前：47）、下院で257（同 214）と大幅に議席を伸ばした見込みだ。CNNによる投票者調査
（事前調査と出口調査による集計：Voter polls）に基づくと、今回の選挙結果の背景として下記
3 点を指摘できる。なお、2024 年大統領選との比較に関してはサンプル規模など調査手法が異な
る点に留意が必要である。

① 若者や中南米系が共和党から離反 
 共和党は白人男性層に支持が多い一方、女性や若者は相対的に民主党を支持する傾向が強い。
2024 年大統領選と比べると、今回の中間選挙ではあらゆる属性で民主党の得票率が伸びており、
特に 18～29 歳の若者層や中南米系で上昇幅が大きい（図表 1）。 これらの有権者層は所得が相対的
に低く、住宅所有率も低いため、物価高に対するトランプ政権への不満が投票行動に反映されや
すかったとみられる。

② 物価高に対する不満 
「生活費の問題が投票に重要（主要）な影響を与えた」との回答は 89%に達し、経済や物価高の
問題が選挙における最大の争点の一つであった。なお、生活費以外の投票要因では政府の汚職が
90%に達し、イラン戦争は 74%、移民取締政策が73%、AI・データセンターが 56%と続く。なお、汚
職への高い回答割合は物価高への不満によって増長された可能性があり、2028 年大統領選でもこ
れが重要な争点となるかは不透明感が残る。 
経済運営を巡って、2024 年大統領選の際には 53%の有権者が「トランプ氏の経済運営の方が信
頼できる」と回答するなど（民主党・ハリス氏は 46%）、トランプ大統領の再選の背景には生活改
善への強い期待があった。一方、今回の中間選挙では「トランプ政策で経済環境が悪化した」と
の回答が65%に達するなど、高関税政策による輸入物価の上昇やイラン戦争によるガソリン価格高
騰への強い不満がみられた。

### Page 2

2/3 
③ 消去法的な民主党への投票 
「民主党を好ましい」とみる有権者は 47%（共和党：同 41%）と、「好ましくない」の 53%を下回
っている。また、全有権者の 18%は「民主党をやや好ましくない」と回答したものの、このうち
46%が民主党候補に投票している。すなわち、全有権者の8%程度は消去法的に民主党候補を選んだ
様子がみてとれる。加えて、全有権者の12%は「社会主義をやや好ましくない」と回答した一方、
このうち71%は民主党候補に投票している。このため、2028 年大統領選までに民主党が左派色を
強める場合、こうした投票者層は再び共和党へ投票先を変更する可能性がある。

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
3/3

・今後の政策見通し 
 民主党は上下院で勝利したものの、トランプ大統領は議会が可決した法案への拒否権を有して
いる。拒否権を覆すには上下両院でそれぞれ 3 分の 2 以上の賛成が必要であり、共和党議員の大
幅な造反がなければ実現は難しいだろう。一方、トランプ政権は議会との対立が強まるなか、大
統領令を活用すると同時に、大統領権限が強い外交や関税政策でのレガシー作りを目指すとみら
れる。イラン戦争は議会による国防予算への権限、関税政策は司法判断がどの程度の歯止めにな
るかが注目される。他方、AI規制は連邦レベルでの規制案が合意に達するか不透明であり、州・
地方政府レベルでのデータセンター建設規制等が続く可能性がある（詳細は 2026 年 10 月28 日付
け「米国：民主党勝利で政策は変わるのか？」）。

米国：高水準保つ非製造業と根強いインフレ圧力（2026 年 10 月 ISM） 
～FRB の 12 月追加利上げ・高金利長期化シナリオを補強、ドル高圧力に～ 
 経済調査部 主任エコノミスト  桂畑 誠治 
（℡：050-5474-7493）

要旨 
・2026 年 10 月のISM非製造業景気指数は 54.5 となり、前月（54.9）から小幅に低下したも
のの、景気拡大の分岐点である 50 を大きく上回る高水準を維持した。トランプ政権の関税政策
による輸入コスト高や地政学リスク等の不確実性を抱えつつも、堅調な米個人消費など強靭な内
需が非製造業部門の活発な動きを力強く支えている。 
・需要の強さと供給制約（入荷遅延指数の上昇）を背景に、インフレ指標である仕入価格指数は
73.0 と著しい高水準で推移した。価格上昇を報告する業種が全体の 9 割に達するなど、人件費
高騰と関税・物流コストが重なるサービスインフレの粘着性が極めて顕著である。 
・9 月の利上げ実施に続き、今回の結果はFRBによる 12 月FOMCでの追加利上げおよびターミ
ナルレート（最終到達金利）の上昇を伴う政策金利の高止まり（Higher for Longer）観測を一
段と強める材料となる。金融市場では、米実質金利の上昇とドル高圧力が強まる一方、資本コス
ト上昇に伴う株式市場のマルチプル（PER）修正圧力となる公算が大きい。

1．拡大ペースは小幅減速も、堅調な国内需要を背景に高水準を維持 
2026 年 10 月のISM非製造業景気指数（季節調整値）は 54.5 となり、前月の 54.9 から 0.4 ポイ
ント低下し、市場予想中央値（54.8）を僅かに下回った。拡大ペースは小幅な鈍化を示唆したも

### Page 2

2/8 
のの、拡大縮小の分岐点である 50 を大きく上回る高水準を維持している。 
トランプ政権による関税賦課への警戒感、エネルギー価格の高止まり、物流混乱が継続するな
か、中東情勢をはじめとする地政学的な先行き不確実性が強いままである。こうした環境のな
か、底堅い米国内需要が強力な下支えとなっているほか、価格上昇や供給懸念を背景とした前倒
し発注の動きも寄与しており、指数自体は高水準を維持している。地政学リスク下にあっても、
米経済の 7 割以上を占める非製造業部門の活動は極めて活発である。

2．企業コメントに見る「地政学リスク」と「業種間の二極化」 
企業からの定性コメントをみると、全般的に需要や事業活動は堅調さを保っているものの、「燃
料高」「人手不足・人件費高」「関税」「サプライチェーンの停滞」によるコスト増加を全産業共通
の課題として指摘する声が上がっている。多くの業界でコスト転嫁が追いつかず利益率の低下や
予算の精査を余儀なくされており、企業活動には慎重姿勢が広がっている。また、強い価格決定
力を持ちコスト転嫁を進める大手企業と、コスト高を吸収しきれない中小企業との間で業種内・
企業間のK字型二極化が鮮明化している。 
業種別にみると、建設・公益分野では、豊富な発注残や旺盛な発電需要に支えられて事業活動
自体は堅調に推移している。しかしながら、一部設備における早期調達の必要性や各種規制の存
在に加え、長期金利の上昇に伴う資金調達コストの増加が懸念材料となっている。 
卸売・小売・情報通信分野においては、通商法 301 条などの関税政策や海上運賃の高騰、さら
にはエルニーニョをはじめとする異常気象がサプライチェーンに深刻な乱れをもたらしている。
こうしたコスト増加を販売価格へ十分に転嫁できず、利益率が圧迫される事例も散見される。 
宿泊飲食・農林水産分野では、ウクライナ情勢の長期化や干ばつの影響による燃料・肥料価格
の高騰が、小麦をはじめとする原材料価格を前年比 30％増へと押し上げている。その結果、実質
購買力の低下などを背景とした需要の低迷が一部でみられる。 
教育・金融保険分野では、施設維持費やIT関連費用、業務委託コストの増加が経営の打撃とな
っている。特にAI導入に関しては、単なる投資拡大のフェーズから、総保有コストや費用対効果
を厳格に管理・評価する段階へとシフトしている。 
企業支援（経営管理・サポートサービス）分野では、事業そのものは堅調さを維持しているも
のの、関税や燃料費の高騰、納入リードタイムの長期化といった要素が徐々に実務上の負担とな
っている。

3．内需の強靭さと供給制約のコントラスト 
10 月の詳細動向をみると、需要側の力強さと供給側の調整・制約の混在が明瞭に表れている。
需要・雇用の面では、新規受注が 55.9（前月比▲3.9 ポイント）、事業活動が 53.8（前月比▲2.7
ポイント）とともに前月より低下し、総合指数に対して事業活動（▲0.68 ポイント寄与）、新規受
注（▲0.98 ポイント寄与）が押し下げ要因となった。ただし、新規受注は長期間にわたり拡大圏
を維持し、受注残指数も 50 を大幅に上回っており、需要超過に伴う手持ち工事・受注の積み上が

りがうかがえる。新規受注や事業活動は政策や地政学リスクの不確実性を背景に月ごとの変動が
見られるものの、トレンドとしての平均値は切り上がっており、サービス需要の基調的な強さを
裏付けている。 
雇用指数は 51.4（前月比＋1.3 ポイント）と拡大圏で上昇した。雇用指数の回復は労働市場全
体としては底堅さを保っていることを示唆する。総合指数への寄与度で見ても、雇用（＋0.32 ポ
イント）が押し上げ要因となっている。ただし、業務効率化や構造変化に伴う慎重な採用姿勢、
および労働力のミスマッチにより、採用ペースは緩やかな伸びにとどまっている。

一方、供給制約とコスト高の面では、入荷遅延指数は 56.7（前月比＋3.5 ポイント）と上昇
し、サプライチェーンの混乱（納期遅延）を示した。慢性的なドライバー不足や、グローバルサ
プライチェーンの部分的な滞留を受け、入荷遅延が再び上昇（悪化）し始めている。入荷遅延の
上昇は通常需給の引き締まりを意味するため、総合指数に対しても入荷遅延の拡大が＋0.88 ポイ
ントの押し上げに作用しているものの、表面の数字ほど需要が強いわけではないことを割り引い
て解釈する必要がある。 
仕入価格は高止まりしており、根強いインフレ圧力が企業の利益率や家計の購買力を圧迫し続
けている。インフレ指標である仕入価格指数は 73.0（前月 74.0）へと前月比 1.0 ポイント低下し
たものの、70 を超える著しい高水準にとどまっている。価格上昇を報告した業種は 17 業種（前月
17 業種）と全 18 業種の大半を占め、価格下落を報告した業種は 8 カ月連続で皆無となった。サー
ビス業における人件費の上昇に加え、エネルギー価格の上昇や貿易関税のコスト転嫁が二次的イ
ンフレ圧力として顕在化している。 
価格が上昇した品目は、銅、メモリー製品、燃料、ガソリン、ソフトウェアライセンス、ディ
ーゼル燃料、鉄鋼製品、石油製品に加え、食品、電線・ケーブルなど多岐にわたる。メモリー部

おいても供給が不十分となっている。

4．業種別の広がり（拡大を報告した業種は 18 業種中 15 業種） 
10 月に総合（景況動向全体）で拡大を報告した業種は、18 業種中 15 業種（前月 13 業種）と増
加し、景気拡大の広がりを維持している。 
拡大した業種は、勢いの強い順に、運輸・倉庫、卸売、情報、小売、芸術・娯楽・レクリエー
ション、金融・保険、その他サービス、不動産・賃貸・リース、宿泊・飲食サービス、農林水産
業、公益、教育サービス、事業支援サービス、専門・科学・技術サービス、公務・行政と続い
た。一方、縮小した業種は、建設、医療・社会扶助の 2 業種（前月 4 業種）に減少した。鉱業は
前月から横ばいとなった。

5．10-12 月期の基調は小幅減速にとどまり巡航ペース維持  
米国経済全体の景気動向を示す「製造業と非製造業を合算したISM総合景気指数」は、10 月に
54.6 と前月から 0.3 ポイント低下し、米国経済全体の拡大ペースの小幅な減速を示した。 
前四半期からの変化をみても、10 月の製造業が 55.5（7-9 月期平均比＋0.6 ポイント）へ上昇
した一方、非製造業が 54.5（同▲0.3 ポイント）へ低下した。これにより、総合景気指数も 54.6
（同▲0.2 ポイント）へ小幅に低下したものの、依然として高水準を維持しており、米経済が巡航
速度を超える安定拡大を続けていることを示している。 
2026 年 10-12 月期の米経済は、関税圧力や高インフレといったコスト面での逆風を抱えつつ

ＩＳＭ景気指数の推移

製造業

も、堅調な個人消費など強靭な国内需要に支えられ、拡大基調を維持する見込みである。10–12 月
期の実質GDP成長率（前期比年率）は、7-9 月期からの大幅な失速を回避し、潜在成長率（1.8～
2.0％）を上回る 2.0％台半ばの堅調な伸びとなる公算が大きい。

6．金融政策・市場への含意（FRBの追加利上げと市場シナリオ） 
FRBが 9 月に利上げを実施した背景にはサービスインフレの根強さがあったが、仕入価格指数
（73.0）の高止まりと強固な内需は、FRBが 12 月FOMCでの追加利上げに踏み切る強い根拠を与え
る。インフレ沈静化に向けた政策金利の引き上げ幅やターミナルレートの上方修正が強く意識さ
れよう。 
債券市場では、9 月に続く追加利上げおよび高金利長期化（Higher for Longer）の織り込みが
進むことで、短期ゾーンを中心に利回りが上昇し、イールドカーブはベア・フラットニングの圧
力を受けやすい。また、実質金利（10 年物TIPS利回り）の上昇が全般的な金融環境を引き締めよ
う。為替市場では米経済の相対的な力強さを背景にドル買い・他通貨安のモメンタムが強まりや
すい。 
株式市場では、金利上昇圧力が再燃することは、特に高バリュエーションなグロース株を中心
に資本コストを押し上げ、株価収益率（PER）の抑制要因となる。ただし、非製造業の堅調さに裏
打ちされた企業業績の底堅さは株価の下支え要因となるため、株式市場は「金利高によるPER抑
制」と「好業績によるEPS拡大」が交錯する神経質な相場展開をたどる可能性が高い。

Document 756: 経済財政諮問会議（2026年10月29日）解説 〜予算編成の抜本見直しの具体化、ＡＩによる経済・社会の変革を見据えた政策形成に向けて～

Source PDF: all report pdf/経済財政諮問会議（2026年10月29日）解説 〜予算編成の抜本見直しの具体化、ＡＩによる経済・社会の変革を見据えた政策形成に向けて～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-04
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

Economic Trends

2026 年 11 月４日

1/5 
経済財政諮問会議（2026 年 10 月29 日）解説 
〜予算編成の抜本見直しの具体化、ＡＩによる経済・社会の変革を見据えた政策形成に向
けて～ 
 経済調査部 首席エコノミスト 永濱 利廣 
（℡：050-5474-7492）

（要旨） 
○ 令和9 年度予算を「責任ある積極財政元年」と位置づけ、成長投資の予見可能性と政策効果を高
めるための以下の 3 つのアプローチが示された。①『「強く豊かな日本」投資枠』の活用、②当
初予算中心の運営への転換、③税収見積もりの精緻化と財源確保。 
○ AI を前提とした社会仕組みの再構築に向け、以下の4 つの視点から包括的な対応が提案された。
①生産性向上と労働市場対応、②セキュリティの強化、③自律性・独立性の確保、④変化への対
応と教育改革。 
○ 筆者提言は以下の通り。 
① 税収推計手法の抜本的精緻化: 成長型経済への構造変化に伴い税収が決算で大きく上振れて
いる実態を踏まえ、乖離要因を正確に分析して精度を高めるべき。 
② 外為特会の余剰金・留保資金の活用: 円安等でバッファーが十分なため、古い留保ルールを
見直し、フローの剰余金全額を一般会計財源へ継続活用する。過剰な留保資金の扱いについ
ても海外事例を参考に再検討すべき。 
③ マクロ生産性の向上と分配構造への目配り: AI 導入だけでなく組織改革・労働移動を促す。
また、資本深化による労働分配率低下や富の集中に伴うリスクを警戒すべき。 
④ 日本型AI 雇用対策の構築: 日本では新卒層よりも「事務職の女性」「非正規雇用層」「ミド
ル・シニア層」が AI 省力化の影響を受けやすいため、これらの層を重点対象としたリスキリ
ングやセーフティネットの構築を推進すべき。

１．はじめに 
2026 年10 月 29 日に開催された経済財政諮問会議では『予算編成の抜本見直しの具体化』と『ＡＩに
よる経済・社会の変革を見据えた政策形成に向けて』について議論が繰り広げられた。 
そこで本稿では、会議資料を基に、諮問会議で展開された内容と筆者の意見を紹介する。

### Page 2

2/5
２. 予算編成の抜本見直しの具体化
令和９年度予算を「責任ある積極財政元年」と位置づけ、骨太方針 2026 で示した予算編成の抜本見
直しを具体化・実行するために３点提言された。これは、危機管理投資や成長投資の予見可能性を高
め、持続的なイノベーションと経済成長を引き出すことを目的とする。
１点目が、『「強く豊かな日本」投資枠』を成長戦略の実行につなげる予算システムとすることで
ある。
具体的には、複数年度の財政措置と実行計画の一体化として、年末に策定される「日本成長戦略実
行計画」の重点分野を起点とし、初年度だけでなく後年度の官民投資規模や支援方針を明らかにして
民間投資の予見可能性を確保すべきとされた。そして、特に経済安全保障上重要な分野等は、特別会
計等で複数年度の十分な財源を確保すべきとされた。
また、「金額を削る」から「政策設計を磨く」予算査定への転換として、単なる単年度の要求額調
整にとどめず、民間投資の誘発効果や複数年度の必要性、後年度の財政負担などを検証して政策の質

を高めるべきとされた。
さらに、専門的知見の活用とポートフォリオ運営の観点から、政府の専門性を高め、外部の専門的
知見も活用しながら進捗管理や効果検証を行うべきとされた。そして、投資枠全体や補助金・税制等
の政策手段を「政策ポートフォリオ」として運営し、効果の乏しいものは縮小・退出して効果の高い
分野へ再配分すべきとされた。
2 点目が、補正予算への恒常的な依存から脱却し、複数年度の予算運営への転換である。
具体的には、当初予算中心の運営として、継続的に必要な施策は当初予算で措置し、補正予算は災
害・突発的事態や経済・物価情勢の急変への対応に限定すべきとされた。
また、複数年度の予見可能性を支える制度として、国庫債務負担行為や基金などを事業の性質に応
じて適切に活用すべきとされた。
そして3 点目が、当初予算中心の運営を支える税収見積もりと財源の確保である。
具体的には、税収見積もりの精緻化として、過去の乖離要因を検証し、内閣府のマクロ経済見通し
とも連携しながら見積もりの精度と透明性を高めるべきとされた。

また、税外収入の確実な確保の観点から、外為特会の財務状況などを踏まえ、活用可能な税外収入
を的確に把握・確保して財源に充てるべきとされた。

### Page 3

3/5
３．ＡＩによる経済・社会の変革を見据えた政策形成に向けて
AI の普及が経済社会構造に大きな変革をもたらすことを踏まえ、AI を単なる一分野としてではな
く、社会の仕組みそのものを設計し直す前提として捉え、包括的な議論と政策対応を進めるために４
点提言された。
1 点目が、ＡＩによる生産性の向上と労働市場への対応である。
具体的には、生産性の最大化の観点から、売上拡大・費用縮小による付加価値の拡大と労働時間の
縮小の両面からAI を活用し、日本が「AI 利用先進国」となることを目指すべきとされた。そして、AI
for Science の強化やスタートアップの育成を加速させるべきとされた。
また、雇用・人材育成・セーフティネットの観点から、労働環境の変化に応じた処遇への反映や、
裁量労働制の拡充を含む労働法制・規制改革の議論を急ぐべきとされた。そして、Job Tag の活用によ
るマッチング機能の強化や、離職者向けの経済的補償制度の導入など流動性の高い労働市場に適した
セーフティネットを整備すべきとされた。

さらに、制度・指標の整備の観点から、AI エージェント等の新しい市場慣行に対応した取引・法制
度の検討を進め、AI 利用コストを含めて付加価値を適切に評価できる指標を整備すべきとされた。
2 点目が、セキュリティの強化についてである。具体的には、AI を使ったサイバー攻撃や不正アク
セスによる個人情報漏えいなどに備え、事案情報の国家サイバー統括室への集約やウェブシステムの
適切な管理、サプライチェーンを通じた侵害対策、データ管理など、官民一体でサイバーセキュリテ
ィを強化すべきとされた。
3 点目が、自律性・独立性確保についてである。具体的には、「フロンティアモデル」を保有する
国・企業に従属しないよう、半導体やフィジカルAI 等のキーコンポーネントにおいて日本企業が必要
不可欠な存在となるような強化を行うべきとされた。同時に、国内での一定の技術力維持や、特定の
AI モデルに依存しない代替性の確保、BCP 対応を進めるべきとされた。
そして4 点目が、ＡＩがもたらす変化への柔軟な対応と教育改革についてである。具体的には、ダ

ボス会議やグローバル AI サミットなど海外の産学官の議論に積極的に参加し、経済財政諮問会議でも
マクロ経済への影響や中長期の姿について包括的な議論を加速させるべきとされた。
また、学校教育においては、知識の習得や演算など AI の得意領域を学ぶ従来の枠組みを抜本的に見
直し、「新しいスキルを学ぶ力」や自ら目的を設定する力を育む教育へと改革すべきとされた。そし
て、OECD 加盟国中最下位となっている学校での AI 利用頻度を改善するため、ガイドラインの不断の更
新等を通じてAI を活用できる環境を整えるべきとされた。

### Page 4

4/5 
４.筆者提言 
以上を踏まえ、本会議において筆者は「予算編成の抜本見直し」と『AI による経済・社会の変革を
見据えた政策形成』の観点から提言を行った。 
まずは、税収見積りと決算の乖離解消についてである。具体的には、直近4 年間の平均で、所得税
は 2.3 兆円、法人税は 1.6 兆円も当初の見積りに対して決算が上振れていることを指摘し、2020 年代
以降、賃金・物価が上昇する成長型経済へ移行したことで、経済と税収の関係に構造変化が起きてい
ると指摘した。そして、この実績を単なる特殊要因とせず、経済構造の変化を正確に把握した上で、
現行の税収推計手法を抜本的に精緻化すべきと主張した。

（出所）内閣府 
続いて、外為特会からの繰入れと財源の継続活用についてである。具体的には、これまでの累積積
立額が3 割に達するまで剰余金を留保するルールについて、円安等による外貨資産の円建て評価上昇
により、含み益を含めた実質的なバッファーは 45%と3 割を大きく超えていると指摘した。そして、こ
うした古い基準にこだわらず、フローの剰余金はその一部ではなく全額を一般会計の財源として、来
年度予算だけでなく以降も継続的に活用すべきと提案した。また、中長期的には実質4 割を超えてい
る過剰な留保資金の扱いについても再検討し、G7 各国の資産・債務管理の在り方を研究して抜本的な
見直しを行うよう促した。

### Page 5

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
5/5

（出所）内閣府 
一方、AI による経済・社会の変革を見据えた政策形成については、マクロの生産性向上に向けた構
造変化を促した。具体的には、データセンターや半導体への設備投資が爆発的に増えている中、それ
が個別の業務効率化にとどまらずマクロレベルの生産性向上につながるかどうかが分岐点であると指
摘し、そのため単なる AI 導入だけでなく、組織改革や労働移動といった構造変化をどう促すかが重要
であると述べた。 
また、長期的な分配構造の変化への懸念として、AI による資本深化が進むことで、長期的には付加
価値が労働よりも資本に集中的に分配され、労働分配率がさらに低下する懸念を指摘した。そして、
富の集中による将来的な消費低迷や自然利子率の押し下げ圧力に注意が必要であると訴えた。 
さらに、AI 時代における雇用・リスキリングのセーフティネット構築として、海外では若年層・新
卒者の雇用がAI で圧迫される傾向が見られる一方、日本においては状況が異なると分析し、日本で
は、事務職を担う女性や非正規雇用層、社内で余剰感のあるミドル・シニア層が AI による省力化・自
動化の影響を最も強く受けるリスクが高いと指摘した。そして、AI 時代に向けた制度再設計では、新
卒や高度人材の育成だけでなく、影響を受けやすい女性や非正規雇用層等に対するリスキリング支援
や労働移動のセーフティネット構築にも目配りする必要があると主張した。

Document 757: 経済財政諮問会議（2026年11月11日）解説 〜今後のマクロ経済運営、成長志向型で持続可能な社会基盤の強化～

Source PDF: all report pdf/経済財政諮問会議（2026年11月11日）解説 〜今後のマクロ経済運営、成長志向型で持続可能な社会基盤の強化～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-11-17
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

Economic Trends

2026年 11月 17日

1/5 
経済財政諮問会議（2026年 11月11日）解説 
〜今後のマクロ経済運営、成長志向型で持続可能な社会基盤の強化～ 
 経済調査部 首席エコノミスト 永濱 利廣 
（℡：050-5474-7492）

（要旨） 
⚫ 経済・物価とマクロ経済運営では、名目 GDP700兆円が視野に入るなど成長への変化が見られ、日
銀は基調的物価上昇率 2%の達成を見据え金融緩和を調整する方針。一方、個人消費の弱さや食料
品高への対策、コスト増分の価格転嫁の徹底が課題。 
⚫ 教育力・研究力の強化面として、AX（AIトランスフォーメーション）や人口減少に対応するため、
初等中等から大学・大学院まで一貫した人材育成改革を進めるべきとされた。学校の AI環境整
備（884億円）やリスキリング推進（702億円）、科研費・運営費交付金の拡充などを報告。 
⚫ インフラ整備と国土強靱化の面からは、「危機管理投資」と「成長投資」を両輪とし、産業クラ
スターを支えるインフラ整備や、予防保全型メンテナンスへの転換、建設業の処遇改善・生産性
向上を推進すべきとされた。 
⚫ 筆者の提言としては主に3点。1点目が財政指標の正当な評価の発信として、OECD基準では日本
の純債務残高対 GDP比は G7内で良好な水準にあり、客観的データの発信による財政の信頼向上
が重要とした。2点目が高齢者消費に関する日銀分析への慎重論として、クレカデータに基づく
日銀の分析は現金派の多い高齢者の実態や総務省の調査（30〜50代の消費低迷）と矛盾するた
め、年金減を売上不振の要因と断定すべきではないとした。3点目が無形資産投資と相続税評価
の見直しに関して、AI・デジタル等の無形資産投資を促すための財政法見直しの議論や、企業の
成長・設備投資を阻害しない非上場株式の相続税評価の早急な方針明確化が必要とした。

１．はじめに 
2026年 11月 11日に開催された経済財政諮問会議では、『今後のマクロ経済運営』と『成長志向型で
持続可能な社会基盤の強化に向けて』について議論が繰り広げられた。 
そこで本稿では、会議資料を基に、諮問会議で展開された内容と筆者の意見を紹介する。

２. 経済・物価の現状と今後の運営方針 
まず経済の現状として、名目GDPで700兆円が視野に入り、3年連続で５％超の賃上げ、就業者数の
過去最多更新など成長型経済への「確かな変化」が見られるとされた（図表１）。

### Page 2

2/5 
図表１ 経済面での前向きな変化（出所：内閣府）

また、物価・金融政策面では、日銀は基調的な物価上昇率が「２％の目標と概ね整合的」との認識
を示しており、政府・日銀が連携しつつ、経済・物価・金融情勢に応じて適切に金融緩和の度合いを
調整していく方針とされた（図表２）。
